Whose band it is, is the first trainer's by class and name: `SceneTransition.Bands` (thirteen `BattleBand`s: base, deep, light, and for two a colour of their own for the card behind the portrait, `Backing`; Kanto's thirteen are `KantoBands` since plan 19 · K8, the Champion's entry named `{rival}` and found by the rival's name; Hoenn's `HoennBands` since plan 21 · H9, part b, looked through before Kanto's), `BandOf`, and `BandFor(meeting)`; a Leader the table doesn't name wears `PlainBand`, and a test holds the table against `trainers.json` both ways. **The portraits** are `UI/BandPortraits.cs` (the engine's `band`): `StartTrainerBattle` begins it after `StartTransition` with the look the battle itself will draw the trainer in (`CharacterStyles.InBattle(trainer)`, the one answer for the rig built behind the fade, the band and the battle's platform; a test holds it equal to `BattleAnimator.EnemyTrainer`) and the player's own (`PlayerIdentity.Character`) in the League's; `GameEngine.Draw` renders them in its offscreen phase, right after `PokemonSprites.Service` and before the scene, each **once** into an 880-pixel target of its own with `CharacterStudio.RenderBust` (framed by `CharacterRig.Face`, lit from the front) in the first frame of the closing that finds its rig ready; `SceneTransition.Draw` is handed a `BandPicture` and lights a portrait by how it draws the one picture (its shape in one flat colour through a small shader while a silhouette); and the midpoint, or the next transition, frees the targets (`End`). **A band never waits for a model**: the renderer asks `CharacterModels.TryGet` and nothing else, and until a rig is there the band shows a plain bust, so whatever starts a band needs no rig ready. `SceneTransition.WarmBands` makes the shader and the mark's lettering in the band's first frame, under the flash. A new band (another region's Leaders) is a row of `Bands` and of the style guide's table. A tool asks the `Driver` for `Band`, `BandTheirLook`, `BandOwnLook`, `BandRendered`, `TransitionSeconds` and `TransitionClosing`; the harness's `BandLit` and `ShotBand` (`Harness.cs`) shoot a band where a mode's script plays it.
- **The legendary's and the mythical's** (plan 09 · L14; style guide, "Life", "Into battle", "The legendary's and the mythical's"; `docs/mechanics/rulings.md`, "The legendary's and the mythical's ways in"): a wild Pokémon's species has its row before the check for two at once (`BattleMeeting.SpeciesRow`, the original's `EncEffects_WildPokemonEffect`, by the species' name whatever the form; the Regis and the Kanto birds are met as anyone is in `BattleKind.PalPark`), and `ForBattle` gives Dialga, Palkia, Uxie, Azelf and Arceus `TransitionKind.Legendary` and Shaymin, Giratina, the Regis, Regigigas, Heatran, Darkrai and Rotom `Mythical`; Mesprit, Cresselia and the birds are met the place's way. **The two are the only ways that move the field's camera**, and they move it through the camera kit's rig: `SceneTransition.LensAt(kind, seconds)` (`UI/SceneTransition.Lenses.cs`, GPU-free) is a `TransitionLens` (a factor over the rig's own zoom, a step of the point looked at in whole texels, the widest factor of the closing), the legendary's widened to 0.85, held and rushed in to `CameraRig.TightestZoom`, the mythical's sixteen lenses of the original's table each taken at once for its frames; `GameEngine.DriveTheLens` hands it to the rig every frame of the closing (`CameraRig.Hold`) and lets go at the midpoint, before the battle is made, and at every `StartTransition` (`LetGo`). **A held lens is not the zoom**: `CameraRig.Lens` (the zoom times the held factor; the zoom itself, to the bit, while nothing is held) is what `Fov`, `LensOf`, `ZoomSlack` and the shadow's reach read, `WidestAhead` makes the held widest ready, and `HeldStep` is added to the camera's position and target by `WorldRenderer` after it is built, so `Look` and everything a script's camera does are untouched. Both close to `SceneTransition.White` as a shape (`ClosesToWhite`, `CoverOf`: a disc from the middle to the furthest corner for the legendary, with a wash over the whole picture, `Wash`, to 200; a frame closing in from the edges for the mythical), which `CoversByWay` and `DrawDark` answer for alike, and the battle opens on the same shape in reverse. The harness's `life` ends with a Dialga and a Giratina (`l36_legendary_*`, `l37_mythical_*`) and prints the lens at each shot.
- **The Galactic mark** (plan 09 · L13, decision 6; style guide, "Life", "The Galactic mark") is `Graphics/GalacticMark.cs` (GPU-free, public): Team Galactic's mark as this game draws it, which is not the original's, in one description for whoever shows it. `Facet`, `MiddleOf` and `ShadeOf` are its six facets as shapes in a space of their own, which the ways into a grunt's and a Commander's battle draw (`SceneTransition.DrawGalacticMark`, `DrawGalacticShard`); `At` says what any point of it is; and `Paint(canvas, cx, top, size, ink, yellow)` lays the same mark down in whole texels at any size. The sign over their buildings' doors is `Paint` at fourteen texels (`BuildingArt.GalacticSign`), and plan 11 · C2's grunt uniforms take theirs from it. A change to the mark is made there and in the style guide, nowhere else.
- **The camera kit** (plan 09 · L4; style guide, "Story scenes", "The camera kit"; `docs/scripts.md`, "The camera kit"): `Overworld/CameraRig.cs` (GPU-free) is the field camera's own moves as one state that is a function of the time handed to `Advance`: where it looks instead of at the player (`Pan` to a spot, `Follow` someone's feet, `Path` through spots at a pace gathered and lost over half a second, `FollowPlayer` to come home), its lens (`ZoomTo`, a factor of 0.5 to 4 eased on its ratio), the cinema bars (`Letterbox`, the title's 120 units) and the field's pace (`SlowMotion`, a share of it for a time, back by itself); `Release` brings all of it back and `Reset` at once. It is `WorldRenderer.Camera`, over whatever camera the place has: `RenderScene` moves it on by the camera's clock (`WorldRenderer.RunCamera`, which the engine runs beside `FieldLife.Advance` at the clock's own pace) and asks `Look` for the target and `LensOf(map)` for the lens, which is `MapScene.ViewOf` with its field of view under the zoom (`CameraRig.Fov`) and nothing else changed, so `SetUpright` and `Straighten`, which go by the pitch and where the camera stands, hold at every zoom. What is in view follows the lens: `VisibleRects` (so `PeopleInSight` too), the chunks made ready (`ChunksUnder` for a lens zoomed out or on its way out, `CameraRig.WidestAhead`), the shadow map's reach, and in a room the camera's leave to look off the room's middle by the share a zoom in took out of the picture (`ZoomSlack`). The engine draws the bars (`DrawLetterbox`, after the script's fade and under the text box) and hands the field `FieldTime(dt)`: the frame's time at the rig's `Pace` for life, scripted walks, gestures, whoever travels along, sliding boulders and a puzzle's pieces, and the clock's own for the camera, the text, bubbles, fades and the runner's waits. Scripts reach it through `camera zoom|follow|path` (`CameraMove`, `IScriptHost.Camera`, which returns how long the move takes: the runner waits that, unless the line ends in `nowait`), `letterbox` and `slowmo` (`Op.Letterbox`, `Op.SlowMotion`; neither is waited for); `EndScript` releases whatever a script left (`cameraSent`), and a new game, a loaded one and a white-out reset it. Anything else that holds the field may drive it (`world.Camera`: the Gyms' clock and bags do through `PanCamera`, plan 09 · L14's ways into a battle will). A pan from a spot the camera was sent to already eases on from there, where it used to jump. Spear Pillar's scene at the altar uses the kit (bars, a path, the lens, slow motion); the Vista Lighthouse's binoculars don't, since their view is of another map (its script's header says what that wants).
- **The title's loop** (plan 09 · L4): `TitlePhase.Idle` goes back to `Journey` after `TitleScreen.IdleTime` (thirty seconds), never to the notice, and `Journey` and `Reveal` play as the first time; three seconds before it `TitleScreen.Render` asks `WorldRenderer.MakeReady` for the first shot's chunks, which bake behind the title. Only `Idle` loops: the menu, a prompt and a notice wait.
- **Field art kit**: buildings, props and furniture are boxes and upright cards whose every face is painted at its exact size. `KitBuilder` measures in texels and emits the geometry; each face is painted on first use into the map's `ArtSheet` (one texture, one batch) by a painter from `BuildingArt` (wall materials, windows, doors, signs, roof tiles, whole façades composed bay by bay, and `BuildingStyle` per kind and town), `OutdoorProps` (lamp, mailbox, planter, bench, signpost and boulder sprites; fences), `PropModels` (room furniture) or `GroundBaker` (room floors and walls). `BuildingModels` shapes the walls and roofs, porches, the tiers of the League and the stages of towers and lighthouses. `BuildingArt.StyleOf` (in `BuildingArt.Styles.cs`) picks the style: a house follows its town (fifteen `Architecture`s, each with its own walls and roof), a public building its kind (twenty-six `BuildingKind`s), and a building of the world brings its storeys, sign and porch; `BuildingArt.Kinds.cs` has the walls, windows and roof pieces the later towns need. `Landmarks` builds the props the world's models place (fountain, ship, wind turbine, statue, honey tree and the rest, and the mine's conveyors, whose belts move like a fountain's water), and ground that is built rather than grown (paving, planks, walkways, stairs) is painted tile for tile by `PixelGround`. `Pix` and `Tone` are the drawing helpers (bevels, a 5×7 alphabet, sprite outlines). All of it, like `NatureArt`, runs without GPU calls, so `BuildingTests` builds every map's kit and checks the art.
- **Characters and the SDF kit**: `SdfModel` sculpts with signed-distance shapes (smooth unions, cuts, paint, each shape with a colour, a `SurfaceMaterial` and a bone); `SdfMesher` meshes it by surface nets (only near the surface, on every core) into an indexed mesh with blended colours and up to four bone weights per vertex; `SdfCache` keeps meshes in `cache/models` next to the executable, keyed by a hash of the model, each file packed (plan 03 · D13: the mesh's own numbers a byte of each at a time and deflated, a third of the size and bit for bit what was meshed; a file from before is still read, and packed as it is read unless `POKEMON_CACHE` shares the folder with other trees). `CharacterModels` builds every look this way (proportions, hair in locks, hats, clothes, a face patch; the normals of face and hair are softened after meshing, so the shade's edge doesn't break up over them) on a 19-bone `Skeleton`, in the background as people come within two chunks of the player (`Preload`, `GameEngine.KeepCharactersNear`), letting go of the rest on a warp (`Trim`). A look is values, not code: `Data/characters.json` (`CharacterStyles`, plan 11 · C1; `docs/data-files.md`, "Characters") holds every look by name, the original's object looks that stand as one of ours for now (`WorldMapBuilder.CharacterFor`) and the look of each trainer class in battle, where the class is drawn and the person's own look (`Trainer.Look`) only for a class that names nobody in particular (a Leader) (`CharacterStyles.InBattle`, `BattleEngine.Show`); `CharacterAnimation` poses it (idle, walk, run, hop, emotes, follow-through); `SkinnedModel` draws it skinned on the GPU, its outline an offset shell of the SDF. The same rig is the 3D trainer in battle (smooth face textures from `CharacterFaces`) and the source of the field sprites. `CharacterStudio` renders turntables for the harness.
- **Battle**: `BattleCore` runs the rules and `BattleEngine` plays its log as a queue of messages ("Battles: the rules and their face", above); `BattleAnimator` holds what the battle looks like at this moment (send-out, lunge, hit, faint, capture, displayed HP, and `CombatantView.Away` for a Pokémon the rules have taken out of sight in the middle of a move: it isn't drawn, and its HP box stays), the **effect cues** the engine starts (`EffectCue`: a move from one place to another and how it landed, or a stat change, heal or status on one Pokémon) and the thrown ball (`BallThrowView`, its timeline in `BallStage`). `BattleRenderer` draws, through the shots of the camera director (`BattleCamera`), the arena's stage (`BattleStage` built by `BattleArenas`, lit by `ArtLook.ArenaRig`, `SkyPainter`, ambient particles from `ArenaFx`), the trainers (who throw with the `Throw` emote), the Pokémon as 3D models from `PokemonModels`, the 3D Poké Balls (`BattleBall`) and the move effects; `BattleHUD` and `ModernUi` draw the interface (a wild foe whose species the Pokédex has as caught shows a Poké Ball after its name, `BattleEngine.ShowsCaughtMark`, as Platinum's health box does; never in a trainer's battle).
- **Move effects**: `MoveFx` turns each cue into quads and ribbons in an `FxList`: a template per type and category, and moves with their own effect looked up by name, built from the primitives in `Fx` (streams, beams, bolts, bursts, shockwaves, auras, rain, contact flashes, slashes, orbs, waves) with shapes from the `FxTextures` atlas. Everything is a pure function of the cue's age and seed (tests check it); `FxRenderer` draws the list inside the 3D pass and `DrawField` adds the screen flash. The arena comes from the ground under the player or the map (`Map.ArenaAt`, `battleArena` in map files). **Moves of their own** (plan 25 · Y6 on; style guide, "Moves of their own"): each batch is a file (`MoveFx.Platinum1.cs`…) whose dictionary `MoveFx.AllRecipes` lays over G8's (`BatchOf`), in the story's order (`MoveSightings` in the test project; plan 25 lists Y7 to Y10); every recipe lands at `ImpactTime` (`FxList.Land`, set by `Impact`), is over by `EffectTime` and draws at most `MoveFx.MaxQuads` (124), which `MovePresentationTests` hold for all 467; its sound is `use_<key>` (`SoundBank.Moves.cs`, `MoveSound(move, type)`), and the moves that are the user's voice play its cry in modes of their own (`SoundBank.CryModesOf`, `BattleEngine.MoveHeard`). A cue carries the battle's ground (`EffectCue.Terrain`, kept by the face, never asked of the rules). New shapes go in the atlas's empty cells (8 × 8), the style guide's shapes line first. A move cast on the user's own side whose look belongs on the foes (Imprison, a weather) asks `FxCtx.Foe` and `FoeSize` (whoever stands across from the user), never `To`, which is the user itself for a self-targeted move (plan 25 · Y7b, `MoveFx.Platinum3.cs`).
- **What lies on the field** (plan 25 · Y2; style guide, "What lies on the field"): `Graphics/BattleFieldFx.cs` (GPU-free) turns `BattleAnimator.FieldLook` into quads and ribbons of the effects' own atlas every frame (`Emit`, which `BattleRenderer.Render` calls after the weather's layer; `Side`, `Field` and `Place` each draw one look from one `ConditionView`): rings of caltrops for Spikes and Toxic Spikes, hovering stones for Stealth Rock, low panes for Reflect and Light Screen, a ring for Safeguard, wisps for Mist, streaks for Tailwind, charms for Lucky Chant, a grid for Trick Room, sinking dust for Gravity (whose light is `ArtLook.UnderGravity`, laid over the rig after the weather's), a star for a Wish and a mote for an attack sent ahead, and since plan 06 · R19b curtains of light for Aurora Veil, a web for Sticky Web and each terrain's wash, band and pieces on both platforms, and since R25 Wonder Room's turning shields and Magic Room's motes, and with its leftovers a rainbow's bands, a sea of fire and a swamp (what two Pledges leave). All of it is our own, since Platinum draws none of it while it lasts. Each look is a pure function of its view's layers, ages and seed and of the battle's clock, sits in the scene (depth-tested) and stays under its `Cap` (the whole field under `MaxQuads`, 533, counting one terrain since the field has one at a time; `BattleFieldLookTests` holds both). Sizes are in the platform's radius, so both sides read alike; what stands up keeps `CameraArc` clear of the way to `BattleStage.CameraPosition`, and what does stand before the camera (the panes) stays under `TallestBeforeTheCamera`, a ninth of the radius, so nothing covers a Pokémon's body. A ribbon may sit in the scene too (`FxList.Ribbon(..., depth: true)`).
- **The turn's moments' looks** (plan 25 · Y3; style guide, "Marks on a Pokémon"): `Graphics/MoveFx.Moments.cs` (`MoveFx` is a partial class) draws a `CueKind.Moment` cue on the Pokémon in its "to" place (`MoveFx.Moment`, one function to a `BattleMoment`; Leech Seed's other end is the cue's "from" place) and the landing half of an attack sent ahead (`EffectCue.Arriving`, `Landing`: Future Sight's falling rings, Doom Desire's pillar, then the move's own `Impact`; `BattleCamera.Direct` goes to its target at once). Every look is our own, a pure function of the cue's age and seed, lands at `ImpactTime`, is gone by `BattleAnimator.MomentTime` (1.1 s) and draws at most `MoveFx.MomentCap` (40) quads and ribbons; none flashes the screen or shakes the camera (`BattleMomentTests` holds each). `Round` (things whirling round a body) and `Bands` (three arcs to a band, `TurnToward` for the one on the camera's side) are how something goes round a body of any width. A held item's cue says whether the item is a berry (`EffectCue.Berry`, which `PlayMoment` reads from the line's own `ItemShown`).
- **The lasting conditions' looks** (plan 09 · L2; style guide, "A condition that lasts"): `Graphics/MoveFx.Conditions.cs` draws what hangs about a Pokémon for as long as its `ConditionLook` has it. `MoveFx.Conditions(anim, places, fx)`, which `BattleRenderer.Render` calls after the cues, adds nothing to the list while nobody has anything; `Ailment` (Z's, bubbles, flames, a paralysed body's fits, a shell of ice), `ConfusionStars` and `LoveHearts` are each a loop in the mark's own age, pure in its age, its strength (0..1, in and out over 0.4 s) and its seed, under `MoveFx.ConditionCap` (6 to 12; 22 for one Pokémon with a condition, confused and in love), and none flashes the screen or shakes the camera (`BattleConditionLookTests` holds each, and that they keep off the middle of the body). `BattleAnimator.LastingShown(side, slot)` is how much of them is drawn: none about a Pokémon behind a doll, out of sight, in its ball, fainting, recalled or caught, and they stand back while a `Moment`, `Status`, `StatUp`, `StatDown` or `Heal` cue plays on that Pokémon, so a moment plays alone. **What stands beside a body stands off its middle by `FxPlaces.HalfWidth`** (half the silhouette's width as the battle turns it, from `SpriteFraming.HalfWidth`, kept between a fifth and three fifths of the height; `BattleRenderer.UpdatePlaces` measures it), what is over a head goes by the height, and the ring round a head by both (`MoveFx.HeadRing`): a new mark that sits beside a body takes the width too. The cast (`MoveFx.Cast`: ice-blue while frozen, a purple pulse while poisoned, a glint as a paralysed body's fit begins) goes through the shader's flash, so it is blended into the model's own colours and costs no quad.
- **The stage's light and the bodies' motions** (plan 25 · Y16; style guide, "Move effects (G8)", "The stage's light", "Bodies that move with a move"): a move that darkens, tints, greys or switches the original's background is a `StageLight` (`Graphics/StageLight.cs`, GPU-free: a colour, an amount, a grey; `StageLights.Table` by move name, 162 of them; `StageLights.Of(anim)` lays the playing cues' over one another, each eased by `Envelope`). `BattleRenderer` paints the sky through it and sets it as two uniforms of the lit programs' fog step (`FieldShaders.SetStageWash`) **for the stage, its lights and the trainers only, and back to `StageLight.None` before the Pokémon**: never a full-screen pass or flash, which would dim the Pokémon too, and the field never sets it. What a move does to a body itself (a rush, a leap, a spin, a squash, a squeeze, a collapse, and a two-turn move's body leaving and coming back: `BattleAnimator.GoAway`, `ComeBack`, `CombatantView.AwayAge`, `BackAge`, `SpentAge`) is a `BodyMotion` (`Graphics/BodyMotion.cs`, GPU-free, in the body's own height) that `DrawPokemon3D` applies for every pass; `BodyMotion.Still` is `new(Grow: 1f)`, never a struct's `new()` (all noughts: a size of nothing), and a still body's matrix must stay the one it was, to the bit. A new move that changes the stage or moves a body is a row of one of the two tables.
- **Pokémon**: `PokemonModels` builds a species' model with `PokeBuilder`, which sculpts with the SDF kit on a skeleton laid out by the species' `BodyPlan` (biped, quadruped, bird, serpent, fish, floating; each bone has a `PokeRole`), and meshes the body and an outline shell through `SdfCache`. All 1,025 species are hand-built: the whole of Platinum's Sinnoh Pokédex, the 24 the story showed first (plan 04) and the rest in `PokemonModels.Sinnoh1.cs` (plan 03 · D6, Kricketot to Lopunny), `PokemonModels.Sinnoh2.cs` (D7, to Hippowdon), `PokemonModels.Sinnoh3.cs` (D8, to Magnezone) and `PokemonModels.Sinnoh4.cs` (D9, the legendaries and Tangela to Absol), the seven of Sinnoh outside it in `PokemonModels.Sinnoh5.cs` (Heatran to Arceus), so every species of Sinnoh, and the popular species from outside it, a batch at a time in National Pokédex order (plan 03, decision 3): `PokemonModels.Kanto1.cs`, Bulbasaur to Vileplume, `PokemonModels.Kanto2.cs`, Paras to Kingler, and `PokemonModels.Kanto3.cs`, Voltorb to Mew, so every species of Kanto, then `PokemonModels.Johto1.cs`, Chikorita to Corsola, and `PokemonModels.Johto2.cs`, Delibird to Celebi, so every species of Johto, then `PokemonModels.Hoenn1.cs`, Treecko to Delcatty, `PokemonModels.Hoenn2.cs`, Sableye to Armaldo, and `PokemonModels.Hoenn3.cs`, Castform to Deoxys, so every species of Hoenn, then `PokemonModels.Unova1.cs`, Victini to Audino, `PokemonModels.Unova2.cs`, Timburr to Zoroark, `PokemonModels.Unova3.cs`, Minccino to Chandelure, and `PokemonModels.Unova4.cs`, Axew to Genesect, so every species of Unova, then `PokemonModels.Kalos1.cs`, Chespin to Slurpuff, and `PokemonModels.Kalos2.cs`, Inkay to Volcanion, so every species of Kalos, then `PokemonModels.Alola1.cs`, Rowlet to Bewear, `PokemonModels.Alola2.cs`, Bounsweet to Kommo-o, and `PokemonModels.Alola3.cs`, Tapu Koko to Melmetal, so every species of Alola, then `PokemonModels.Galar1.cs`, Grookey to Eldegoss, `PokemonModels.Galar2.cs`, Wooloo to Polteageist, `PokemonModels.Galar3.cs`, Hatenna to Morpeko, and `PokemonModels.Galar4.cs`, Cufant to Calyrex, so every species of Galar, then `PokemonModels.Hisui.cs`, Wyrdeer to Enamorus, the seven first met in Hisui, `PokemonModels.Paldea1.cs`, Sprigatito to Pawmot, `PokemonModels.Paldea2.cs`, Tandemaus to Klawf, `PokemonModels.Paldea3.cs`, Capsakid to Dondozo, `PokemonModels.Paldea4.cs`, Tatsugiri to Gholdengo, with Tatsugiri's looks and their Megas, Dudunsparce's Three-Segment Form, Baxcalibur's Mega and Gimmighoul's Roaming Form, and `PokemonModels.Paldea5.cs`, Wo-Chien to Pecharunt, with Koraidon's builds, Miraidon's modes, Ogerpon's masks and Terapagos's Terastal and Stellar Forms, so every species of Paldea and of the National Pokédex. So are their forms, all listed in `PokemonModels.Forms`: Platinum's own 64 (Rotom's appliances, Giratina's Origin Forme, the cloaks, the East Sea, Cherrim in the sun, Unown's letters, Castform's weathers, Deoxys's formes, Shaymin's Sky Forme, Arceus's types) in `PokemonModels.Forms.cs`, and the later games' 398 in `PokemonModels.Regional.cs` (the regional forms, Dialga's and Palkia's Origin Formes, Basculin's stripes, Darmanitan's Zen Modes, Deerling's and Sawsbuck's seasons, the female Frillish and Jellicent, the Therian Formes, Kyurem's fusions, Keldeo's Resolute Form, Meloetta's Pirouette Forme, Genesect's drives, Ash-Greninja, Vivillon's patterns, the female Pyroar, the Flabébé line's colours and the Eternal Floette, Furfrou's trims, the female Meowstic, Aegislash's Blade Forme, the sizes of the Pumpkaboo line, Xerneas's Active Mode, Zygarde's formes, Hoopa Unbound, the Hisuian Decidueye, Oricorio's styles, Lycanroc's Midnight and Dusk Forms, Wishiwashi's School Form, Silvally's memories, Minior's cores, the busted Mimikyu, Necrozma's fusions and Ultra Necrozma, Magearna's Original Color, Cramorant's catches, the Low Key Toxtricity, Alcremie's creams and sweets, Eiscue's Noice Face, the female Indeedee, the Hangry Morpeko, the Crowned Zacian and Zamazenta, Eternatus's Eternamax, Urshifu's Rapid Strike Style, Zarude's Dada, Calyrex's Ice and Shadow Riders, the Bloodmoon Ursaluna, the female Basculegion, the Therian Enamorus, the female Oinkologne, Maushold's family of three, Squawkabilly's plumages and Palafin's Hero Form), `.Megas.cs` (with the Primal Kyogre and Groudon), `.Gigantamax.cs` and `.Pikachu.cs` (Pikachu's caps and costumes, the spiky-eared Pichu), each file in National Pokédex order. A batch of species brings their forms with it, or they would show the species' sculpt. The forms that look just like their species (Mothim's cloaks, the partner Pikachu and Eevee, Greninja's Battle Bond, Scatterbug's and Spewpa's patterns, which show only once they are Vivillon, the 50% Zygarde with Power Construct, the Own Tempo Rockruff, Minior's meteors, whose cores don't show, the antique Sinistea and Polteageist, the Artisan Poltchageist and the Masterpiece Sinistcha, whose mark is under their foot) show its sculpt, and a form that looks just like another form shows that one's (`PokemonModels.LooksLike`: the 10% Zygarde with Power Construct, the Gigantamax Appletun, which is the Gigantamax Flapple's great apple, and the Low Key Toxtricity's Gigantamax form, the Amped one's). Anything without a sculpt is generated from its data (plan 03 · D5; since the last batch no species is): `PokemonGenomes` turns a species into a `PokeGenome` (one of 18 `BodyKind`s from its Pokédex shape and category words, colours, proportions, parts; family traits seeded by the line's first species) and `PokemonGenerator` sculpts it. A form is a model of its own (`PokemonGenomes.For` takes a form's name: the form's data and colour, its species' family); a form of a hand-built species without a sculpt of its own shows its species'. A glTF file named after the species or the form in `overrides/models` replaces either (`ModelOverrides`, `GltfReader`, `ImportedModels`; the user's guide is [`docs/model-files.md`](docs/model-files.md)): a `PokeModel` with `Imported` set is drawn in pieces of at most 32 bones with its textures, posed by the file's own clips, matched by name, and moved whole by the body plan's for the rest. Eyes and small markings are decals (`PokemonDecals`): the triangles under each are copied with texture coordinates into an atlas painted once per `EyeState`. `PokemonAnimation` poses a model by role (idle, physical, special, status, hit, faint, entry; `BattleAnimator` passes the move's category), `PokemonRenderer` draws it. The same models appear in 3D in battle and the title and are baked by `PokemonSprites` into 2D pixel sprites for menus. Models are built when needed: `PokemonModels.Request` starts one in the background (the game asks for a battle's or an evolution's before the fade and waits for them at its midpoint), `TryGet` never waits, `Get` does, `Trim` lets go of what the next scene doesn't need. No model is kept for good but the stand-in's (plan 03 · D13): the start asks only for those its first screens draw at once (`PokemonModels.ShownAtOnce`: the title's Giratina, the introduction's Buneary) and makes ready the menu sprites of the opening's twenty-four (`PokemonModels.Preloaded`), whose models are made when a scene asks, like every other. A model built only so that a sprite can be baked from it is on loan (`PokemonModels.Borrow`, and `Return` once the baking is done, which lets it go unless the game asked for it meanwhile): whatever bakes from models it has no other use for borrows them, a few at a time (`PokemonSprites.MostOnLoan`), or a walk down the Pokédex keeps a model for every species. A Pokémon standing in the field (plan 10 · F1: `NPC.PokemonType`, `NPC.Species`; the world's objects whose looks are a species, `WorldMapBuilder.SpeciesFor`) is drawn as its field sprite (`FieldSprites`: baked from the model at the people's 32 texels to the tile, four facings of two idle frames on a card of 32, 48 or 64, cached beside the menu sprites; a Poké Ball while it bakes, so the harness sets `FieldSprites.Patient`), runs `common.Pokemon` (`cry own`, `sayown`), and the player surfs on the field sprite of the Pokémon whose Surf it was (`Player.Carrier`). Menu sprites are baked the first time a menu asks for one (`GetBaked` returns null and requests it; `Service` runs once a frame before the 3D passes; the stand-in shows meanwhile) and cached as PNGs in `cache/sprites`, keyed by `PokemonModels.Signature`. **Where one Pokémon is looked at its sprite breathes** (plan 09 · L5; style guide, "Pokémon in menus"): a strip of eight frames of one breath (`PokemonSprites.GetStrip`, `PixelArtGenerator.StripOf`, drawn by `ModernUi.Breathing`; `PokemonAnimation.StripPose` folds the loop so the frame after the last is the first), cached under its own `StripVersion` with the breath in its file name, at most `StripsKept` (4) held; only `DexInfo` (the entry open on its own, once its hop has landed), `DrawSummary` and `DrawStarters` ask for one (`MenuStripTests` scans the sources), never a list, and the harness sets `PokemonSprites.PatientStrips` so a strip is there the frame after it is asked for. `GltfWriter` writes a sculpted model out as `.glb`. `PokemonStudio` renders turntables and clip strips for the harness. Tests keep the hand-built list from shrinking, every species of the Sinnoh Pokédex and every form of a hand-built species hand-built (but those that look the same), every hand-built sculpt distinct and every hand-built model in one piece (`PiecesOf` in `PokemonModelTests` names the few whose parts float apart by design, Shedinja's halo and Hoopa Unbound's free hands among them).
- **Shiny looks** (plan 09 · L1; style guide, "Shiny Pokémon"): a Pokémon's look is `Pokemon.LookName`, its `ModelName` with `+s` when it is shiny (`Models/PokemonLook.cs`: `Shiny`, `Base`, `Of`). **Whatever shows a particular Pokémon asks by its look** (its model, its menu and field sprites, `PixelArtGenerator.IconOf` and `SpriteOf`), and whatever reads the data (a size, a cry, a number) by the base name. `PokemonModels.Build("X+s")` is `X`'s model, its mesh from the same `SdfCache` entry, with a `ShinyPalette` (`Graphics/ShinyPalettes.cs`, GPU-free: the table of hand-picked palettes and the rule by the National number) that `PokemonRenderer.EnsureUploaded` and `PokemonDecals.Paint` put it up in; the mesh and the genome are never changed. `Signature("X+s")` is its own and a plain signature never moves, so no plain sprite is baked again; a change to a palette raises `ShinyPalettes.Version`. A shiny's sparkle is `CueKind.Shiny` (`BattleAnimator.ShinySparkle`, seeded by its own count), raised by the face on `Entered`, never a log event; its star is `UiIcons.ShinyStar` after the name in `ModernUi.NameWithGender`; `Pokedex.CaughtShiny` (saved) gives an entry's FORMS page its shiny card.
- **Female looks** (plan 09 · L6 to L8; style guide, "Female looks"): a female whose model is in `PokemonLook.Females` (`PokemonModels.Females`; GPU-free) is the look `X+f`, before any `+s` (`Pikachu+f+s`; `PokemonLook.Of(model, female, shiny)`, `IsFemale`, `Unshined` the sculpt's name, `Base` the data's). `PokemonModels.Create("X+f")` is `CreateFemale`: the species' own sculptor called with `female: true`, which changes only what differs and builds the male to the bit as before, so a male's mesh and signature never move; her signature is marked as hers. Whatever makes a look from a model name with a Pokémon in hand passes its gender (`PokemonLook.Of(shape, p.Gender == Gender.Female, p.IsShiny)`: an evolution's target, a hatching, a battle's shapes, the save's and the records' icons). `species.json`'s `genderDifferences` (PokeAPI's flag) is what `FemaleLookTests` holds the list to; **a batch that hand-builds a flagged species gives it its female in the same session** (a `female` parameter on its sculptor, a row of `CreateFemale`, a name in `Females`, a row of the style guide's table). The harness's `dex --females` puts each male beside his female. Since L8 all 95 flagged species whose females are no form of their own are sculpted (the other 7, Meowstic and the rest, are `-Female` forms); `PokemonLook.HasFemale` is the list **and** every flagged species without a female form, and one with no sculpt is generated by rule (`PokemonGenomes.For("X+f")` is `FemaleOf` her male's genome: `Female`, the head by `FemaleHeadScale`), so a species the data flags never shows a female as her male. The Pokédex's FORMS page draws a look seen by `SeenLook.Look` (a female in hers), never by its `Name`, which stays the data's.
- **Materials**: `SurfaceMaterials` is the table the character shaders read through the vertex's second texture coordinate (terminator width, highlight band, own light, shade tint). Material 0 is the plain look for models without ids; Pokémon use fur, scales, shell, leaf, metal and glow.
- **Look**: `ArtLook` holds the numbers of the style guide as light rigs (sun, ambient, fog, post settings, sky, how far lamps and home windows are lit) for Platinum's five times of day, blended around each boundary; rooms have rigs of their own that follow the clock through their windows, and a cave has one that ignores it (`CaveFieldRig`; ask `ArtLook.FieldRig(hour, map)`); snow country (an area with more snow than lawn, `MapArea.Snowbound`) grades the hour's rig colder (`ArtLook.Snowbound`, eased in by `WorldRenderer` at an area's border), and a place with a climate of its own (plan 01 · M10, part b: a volcano in the open and inside, a temple of ice; style guide, "Climates") has it laid over its rig the same way (`ArtLook.InClimate`), with what the art marks as lit glowing by day in lava's own colour (`VolcanicGlow`, `LavaGlow`). The dark of an unlit cave is drawn over the composite (`WorldRenderer.DrawDarkness`). `GameClock` gives the hour from the system clock unless the options fix it.
- **Interface**: screens are composed from the kit. `UI/Kit` has `UiFonts` (Nunito), `UiShapes` (anti-aliased SDF panels, rings, lines, triangles), `UiIcons`, and the GPU-free `UiMotion`/`UiReveal` (easing, slide-in), `UiNav` (grid cursors) and `HeldKey` (a key kept down repeating in a long list: the Pokédex, a pocket of the bag, a shop's stock; a held step stops at the list's end where a press wraps, and `CursorTick` keeps its sound from running together). A battle's menus move their cursors in `BattleEngine.MoveCursor`, which follows the way each menu is drawn (the commands are not a grid). `ModernUi` (a partial class: shared components, `.Battle`, `.Party`, `.Field`, `.Lists`, `.Items`, `.Records`, `.Storage`, `.Intro`) holds the colour tokens and components; add a component there rather than drawing shapes in a screen. Each screen keeps its logic in methods that take no input (`StartMenu.Move/Confirm/Cancel`, `TitleScreen.PressConfirm`, `PartyScreen.MoveCursor`) so tests and the harness can drive it. The menu screens (bag, shop, Pokédex, Trainer Card, PC boxes, starter choice, saving, options; style guide, "Menu screens") share the parts in `ModernUi.Lists.cs` (`ListRow`, `Tabs`, `ScrollBar`, `Stepper`, `Money`, `SaveSummary`) and scroll with `UiNav.Window`; a list shows its screen's `VisibleRows`, which a test keeps equal to what its panel holds. What a bag item does outside battle (plan 06 · R11) is `Models/ItemUse.cs` (anything used on one Pokémon, by the item table's use parameters in the original's two passes, `WouldHelp` and `Apply`; PP Ups are `Move.PPUps`, saved and copied with the move), `Models/MoveTeaching.cs` (TMs and HMs by the species' `TmMoves`), `Models/EncounterAids.cs` (Repels and the flutes, which `WildLead` carries into the encounter rules), `Models/Evolution.cs` and `FormRules`; in battle it is `BattleCore.Items.cs` (every medicine and battle item of Platinum's, plan 06 · R8), reached through the battle's own bag (`Battle/BattleBag.cs`: the four pockets by `ItemData.BattlePocket`, and the last item used; `BattleEngine.SelectBagPocket`, `SelectBagItem`, `SelectBagTarget`, `SelectBagMove`, and `UseBagItem` for tests). A Mart's stock is `Data/MartDatabase.cs` (`marts.json`: the common counter by the badges, each town's own by its key, `NPC.Mart`, `shop "key"`); the shop asks BUY, SELL or SEE YA! first (`ShopMode`), and its `Confirm` returns what the money changes by. The helps the newest games made their default (plan 12) are options of `RulesDefault`, on unless turned off since plan 06 · R19 (its middle value, `Rules`, is read from an older settings file as `On`, and the options offer only `Off` and `On`): the first is `MoveHints` (plan 12 · Q10), handed to a battle as `BattleSetup.MoveHints` and answered by `BattleEngine.HintFor`, which the rules never read; the options' last row opens `HelpScreen` (the type chart, the controls from `InputManager.Bindings`, notes). Dialogue is written at the options' text speed in characters a second (`DialogueManager.CharactersPerSecond`, set by `ApplySettings`), whatever the frame rate.
- **Pokédex** (plan 03 · D10, plan 18 · W3): a Pokédex is a PokeAPI identifier (`Pokedex.National`, `Pokedex.Sinnoh` = `extended-sinnoh`, `kanto`…), numbering the species whose `PokemonSpecies.DexNumbers` (`dexNumbers` in `species.json`, from the importer's `AddDexNumbers`) has it; seen and caught are shared by National number. The rules are GPU-free in `Models/Pokedex.cs` (`Entries(dex)`, `Dexes`: the regional Pokédex of each region entered, `Enter(region)`, saved as `SaveData.DexesEntered`, then the National one; `CanUnlockNational(story, region)` plays the region file's `nationalUnlock` rule, `NationalUnlock.Parse`; completion and `AwardDiplomas` by dex, a diploma saved under `Pokedex.DiplomaKey`); `PokedexScreen.Open(pokedex, portrait, here)` and `AreaRegion` draw the AREA page of the listing's own region (`Habitats.Of(region)`), a text key by `Pokedex.KeyOf` and `Models/PokedexSearch.cs` (Platinum's orders, name groups, types and shapes); `Data/Habitats.cs` reads where each species lives. `PokedexScreen` keeps its state (list, entry pages, search panel, diploma) in methods that take no input; `ModernUi.Pokedex.cs` draws it. Species' regional numbers are `PokemonSpecies.SinnohNumber`. **Footprints** (plan 09 · L9; style guide, the Pokédex's "Footprints") are `Graphics/Footprints.cs`: a 16×16 `Footprint` made from the species' own model (one leg's sole seen from below, drawn as its body plan's print, GPU-free `Make`; none for a fish, a floater or a serpent), kept beside the menu sprites by signature; only the opened entry asks for one (`Footprints.Icon`, once its hop has landed), the list and the INFO page beside it show one already made (`HeldIcon`), and the harness sets `Footprints.Patient`. A script asks the lead's friendship with `if friendship >= 200` (Dr. Footstep).
- **Written entries** (plan 08 · P13; design "Written entries" in `docs/plans/08-platinum-details.md`; style guide, "Menu screens", the Pokédex's INFO): `Data/dex-entries.json` is the Pokédex's text, written by hand in our own words and never by the importer: one object keyed by a species' name (later a form's, P17) exactly as `species.json` writes it, in National order, each with two `entries` and a `habitat`, one of Platinum's nine kinds of place (`DexHabitat`: `Grassland`, `Forest`, `WatersEdge`, `Sea`, `Cave`, `Mountain`, `RoughTerrain`, `Urban`, `Rare`; `DexEntries.Word` says them). `Data/DexEntries.cs` reads it once (`Of`, `EntriesOf`, `HabitatOf`, `EntryFor(species, which)`, which falls back to `PokemonSpecies.DexEntry`, the sentence the importer composes, for a name the file lacks). The INFO page shows the two in turn, one more each time an entry is opened (`PokedexScreen.EntryShown`, `Openings`; kept while the game runs, not saved), and HABITAT beside HEIGHT and WEIGHT; the starters' briefcase shows the first. **A batch of entries** (P13 the Sinnoh Pokédex's 210, P14 to P16 the rest by region, P17 the forms) writes in National order, runs `--check-entries` (`tools/DataImporter/EntryCheck.cs`: every run of six words, lower case, letters and digits only, shared with any English flavour text of PokeAPI's `pokemon_species_flavor_text.csv`, which never leaves the git-ignored cache) and writes again whatever it reports until it reports nothing, then raises `DataFileTests.WrittenEntriesThrough` to its last species. The check can't be a test: the games' text must never enter the repository. `DataFileTests.TheWrittenEntriesKeepTheirShape` holds every name a species or form of the data, in National order, two entries of 15 to 45 words that share no run of five words, neither the generated sentence, and a habitat of the nine.
- **The player's own files** (plan 08 · P20; `docs/mechanics/rulings.md`, "Reading the player's own files"): `Foreign/` (GPU-free, and by `ArchitectureTests` free of any write to a file) reads a GBA save (`GbaSave`), a Platinum save (`PlatinumSave`) and a Pokémon file of either console (`.pk3`, `.ek3`, `.pk4`, `.ek4`: `GbaPokemon`, `DsPokemon`) into plain data (`ForeignSave`, `ForeignPokemon`), through one door, `ForeignFile.Open(path)` (a file told by its size, opened read-only, **refused whole** with a `ForeignRefusal` or read whole). `ForeignPokemon.ToSaved` is the original's conversion (`BoxMonGBAToBoxMon`) into `SavedPokemonData`; its tables are `Data/migration.json` (`MigrationDatabase`, from `tools/DataImporter`'s `Importer.Migration.cs`, never by hand). No file of the games enters the repository: `MigrationTests` builds each one byte by byte, and the readers are written from the decompilation's C, never from a tool's. A file read is the same bytes afterwards, and `migrate/` is git-ignored. **Pal Park's migration** (plan 08 · P21; `docs/mechanics/rulings.md`, "Pal Park's migration"; style guide, "Menu screens", "Migrate") is what calls them: `Models/Migration.cs` (GPU-free: Platinum's rules from `gba_migrator.c` and `pal_park_transfers.c`, the legality check and our own "none that came before", the time an argument), `PalParkLedger` in `Models/Pastimes.cs` (`PastimesSave.Sources` and `Came`), `Core/MigrationFolder.cs` (the folder's sources: saves, the loose Pokémon files as one, the refused), `UI/MigrateScreen.cs` and `ModernUi.Migrate.cs` (`GameState.Migrate`, reached from the title's `TitleChoice.Migrate`, offered by `TitleScreen.OfferMigration` only with a readable file and a save) and `Core/GameEngine.Migration.cs`, which reads the slot afresh, brings the six into its park and writes the slot, changing the title's copy only once that was written. Six exactly are chosen; they wait in `PalParkState.Waiting` for the Catching Show. **Between this game's saves, and the way out** (plan 08 · P22; rulings, "Between this game's saves, and the way out"): the title opens the screen on a menu (`MigratePhase.Menu`; the harness's P21 shots open it without), MIGRATE is offered also with two readable saves, and each slot is a source (`ForeignKind.OwnSave`, `Migration.FromSave`, `ForeignPokemon.Own`: its boxes, each Pokémon whole). `Core/SlotMoves.cs` moves six into another slot's park by **four writes with a `MoveReceipt` in both saves** (`SaveData.Receipts`), and **`SlotMoves.Resolve` runs in `ReadTheSlots` before the slots are read**, so that each Pokémon is in exactly one save after a crash at any write (a receiving save that is gone or is another game gets nothing and the six go back); a Pokémon is found by its `Pokemon.Id`, and a move between slots is no `Came`/ledger business, since the six leave the source. **Writing out** is `Core/MigrationOut.cs` into `migrate/out/` (`FileMode.CreateNew`, never over a file): `DsPokemon.Write` (a `.pk4`: 136 plain bytes, only species to 493, later items, abilities and moves written as none) or `Models/ShowdownText.Of` (Showdown's text, English). `migrate/out/` is the one place the game writes in the folder; every reader still opens its file read-only.
- **The map of the region** (plan 01 · M12; `docs/mechanics/rulings.md`, "The Town Map"; style guide, "The Town Map"): one set of data, one set of rules and one screen for the Town Map, Fly's choice, a map on a wall and the Pokédex's area page. `Data/RegionMap.cs` reads a region's `townmap.json` (`docs/data-files.md`; GPU-free; `RegionMap.Of(region)`, `ForMap(mapName)`): a grid with a cell to each chunk of the region's streamed map, the blocks under the cursor with their areas and landmarks and what is said of each, the hidden places, Fly's places, `CellOf` a tile, and `PlacesOf`, which gives every room and cave the cell it is come into from. A region with no file has no map and everything that asks takes a null. `Models/TownMap.cs` is what one game has put on it (`TownMapState`, made as the map opens, from the story: the hidden places revealed, which words a first arrival has earned, Fly's places open, the player's cell and the way come) and `Models/MapTrail.cs` the way come (the original's `OverworldMapHistory`: six cells, written by `OnStep` and by every arrival on the overworld, saved as `SaveData.MapTrail`). `UI/TownMapScreen.cs` is the screen in its three modes (`TownMapMode.Item`, `Fly`, `Wall`), on `GameState.FlyMap`: `Open`, `Move`, `Confirm`, `Cancel`, `ToggleZoom`, `Advance` and `TakeChoice` take no input. `GameEngine.TownMap.cs` is where they meet the game: `OpenTownMap(mode)` (the bag's USE on the Town Map, the item button; a wall's map is plan 08 · P8's to call), `RegionCellNow`, `TownMapNow`, and `NoteArrivalOnTheMap`, which sets a landmark's `FLAG_FIRST_ARRIVAL_…` where the file's `arrival` names the place and no script does. The picture is `Graphics/RegionMapArt.cs` (GPU-free, a texel a tile, from the world's own tiles) kept as two textures by `RegionMapPictures` (painted as a game is entered and again when a hidden place is revealed); `ModernUi.RegionPicture` draws it in its frame for whoever shows the region. Fly lands where `SpawnLocations.FlyFlag` is set (the map's own flag for a place, or the spawn's first arrival), so Pal Park is flown to once its gate has been come to. The original's Town Map marks no roamer and no swarm, and neither does this one: those are the Pokétch's Marking Map's and the assistant's sister's.
- **Who the player is** (`Core/PlayerIdentity.cs`): the name and the look (boy or girl) chosen in the introduction, kept in the save (`SaveData.Look`, beside the trainer ID and the day the adventure began). The character the player isn't is the professor's assistant. Written text never names either: lines, people's names and a map's `displayName` say `{player}` and `{assistant}`, which `DialogueManager` and `Map.DisplayNameAt` fill in (`PlayerIdentity.Fill`); the rival's name, the introduction's last question (`PlayerIdentity.RivalName`, `SaveData.RivalName`, Platinum's own if none is typed), is `{rival}`, and a trainer of the data's `Rival` class is named so as it is filled. The `npcType`s `Player` and `Assistant` become a character through `PlayerIdentity.CharacterFor`. It is static: `StartNewGame(name, look)` and loading a save set it, and tests must not (battle tests read the default name, and test classes run in parallel); they use the overloads that take a name and a look.
- **The introduction** (`UI/IntroScreen.cs`; style guide, "The new-game introduction"): a function of its phase and its clocks (`Appearance()` gives an `IntroLook`) and four buttons (`PressConfirm`, `PressCancel`, `PressStart`, `Move`), so tests play it through. Its 3D figures (the professor, the two player characters, the Pokémon, the ball) are rendered into pictures of their own in `Render`, before `BeginTextureMode(virtualScreen)`, with `CharacterStudio.Render`, `PokemonSprites.Render` and `CharacterStudio.RenderBall`. A name is entered with `UI/Kit/NameEntry.cs` (GPU-free: the keyboard's cursor and rules) and never typed, because the game's own keys are letters.
- **The jukebox** (plan 12 · Q15; style guide, "Menu screens", "Jukebox"): `UI/JukeboxScreen.cs` (GPU-free: four lists built with no window, `Songs(heard)`, `Sounds()`, `Cries()`, `Beds()`; `Move`, `ChangeTab`, `ChangeVariant`, `Confirm`, `Cancel` take no input) drawn by `ModernUi.Jukebox.cs`, state `GameState.Jukebox`, opened by `GameEngine.OpenJukebox(returnTo)` from the title's JUKEBOX row and the options' JUKEBOX row. **A song is unlocked once it has been played** (`AudioManager.Heard`, noted by `PlayMusic` and `PlayFanfare`, saved as `SaveData.Heard`, restored by `SetHeard`); a locked song reads `???` and offers only its day arrangement. Night and low-HP are offered only where `Song.HasNight` / `Song.HasLowHp`. `AudioManager.PreviewMusic` and `PreviewBed` are the jukebox's plays (a test hears `music <id> [night] [lowhp]` and `bed <Name>`). A new sound, song, cry mode or bed appears in it by itself; a new `CryMode` or `AmbienceBed` needs its `jukebox.cry.*`, `jukebox.cryNote.*` or `jukebox.bed.*` words.
- **Music**: songs are MML text files in `Data/music/<region>/` and `Data/music/common/`, played by our own synthesiser (`Audio/`) on raylib's audio thread, and so are the sound effects (`SoundBank`, synthesised on first use with `SoundDesign`; [`docs/sound-effects.md`](docs/sound-effects.md) lists every one, the original's sound it stands for and when it plays, and `SoundTests` holds the code to it: a sound the code names must be in the bank, and tests hear what the game plays through `AudioManager.Listen`) and every species' cry (`Cries`, from its size, name, types and family, in the original's modes; `AudioManager.PlayCry`, the same document's "Cries") and the field's ambience (`Ambience`'s looping beds of weather, water and caves, chosen where the player stands by `Overworld/FieldAmbience` and set every frame with `AudioManager.SetAmbience`; the same document's "Ambience"), all through one `AudioMixer` with a bus and a volume for the music, the fanfares, the effects, the cries and the ambience (the options set each but the fanfares', which follow the music's; `GameSettings.MusicVolume`, `SoundVolume`, `CryVolume`, `AmbienceVolume`), each bus's content held to a loudness window (`Audio/Loudness`), and an option that plays it all as the handheld's speakers would (`GameSettings.Speakers`); `docs/music-format.md` describes the format, the instruments, the mixer and the checks. Maps name their theme in `bgmTrack`; `MusicDirector` picks the riding, eye, battle, victory and fanfare themes by role, looking in the region's folder before `common` and falling back to a more general role (a scene's song that only some regions have, Kanto's credits and Hall of Fame ceremony, is asked of the region's folder by name first: `MusicDirector.SceneSong`, `AudioManager.PlaySceneMusic`; plan 19 · K8); what a trainer class brings (its eye theme, a battle theme of its own) is `Data/audio/sound-map.json`; a wild legendary or mythical Pokémon brings its own battle theme (`Data/music/common/legendary/<key>.mml`, `MusicDirector.WildBattleTheme`). A battle theme has a low-HP arrangement (`lowhp=`, `only=lowhp`, `unless=lowhp`, `lowhptempo`) that the engine switches on mid-song while the player's Pokémon is in the red, Black and White's way rather than Platinum's beep (`AudioManager.LowHp`, `BattleEngine.PlayerInDanger`). Call `AudioManager.PlayMusic` / `PlayFanfare` / `PlaySound`, never the mixer directly. Claude can't listen: render with `tools/MusicRender` (`--sounds` for the effects, `--ambience` for the beds, `--spectrogram` for pictures of the songs), read its level, loudness, click and clash report, look at the spectrograms, and give the user MP3s to approve.
- **Measuring** (plan 04 · G11): `FrameProfiler` (off unless a tool switches it on) splits a frame into its passes; each `Lap` waits for the graphics card to finish (`Gl.Finish`), so a pass's time is the work it caused. `Core/Dice` is where the game's chance comes from when nobody hands a generator of their own, and `FrameClock` is the time the picture's small motions keep (grass, a menu icon's hop): both can be set by a tool, which is how the harness's runs repeat.
- **Leaving the game**: the start menu's QUIT GAME, the title's QUIT and the close button's question (`RequestClose`, plan 12 · Q1) set `GameEngine.QuitRequested`, which alone ends the loop in `Program.cs`. Esc is "back", never "exit".

### Rendering rules that have caused bugs

- raylib texture modes can't nest. Every offscreen pass (field, battle stage, sprite baking, post buffers) must finish before `BeginTextureMode(virtualScreen)`.
- Matrices are built with System.Numerics (row vectors: "A then B" is `A * B`) and must be transposed for `Raylib.DrawMesh`. The light view-projection is `Raymath.MatrixMultiply(view, proj)`.
- rlgl immediate-mode batches don't set `matModel`, which the custom shaders use. Draw immediate-mode quads (speech bubbles, blob shadows) with the default shader.
- Extra samplers set with `SetShaderValueTexture` must be bound after `BeginShaderMode`; each batch flush clears them.
- `WorldRenderer` and `BattleRenderer` share `RenderContext`, so each must set its own uniforms every frame (lighting, character style, fog, time, glow, upright).
- The field's water mirror is a uniform of the shared water program (plan 09 · L10): the field switches it off again after its scene pass, or a battle's water drawn with the same program would read a mirror that isn't bound (its `texture1` falls back to the water's own mask). A uniform the field sets for one pass alone is put back at the pass's end.
- Upright things in the field are stretched by `MapScene.VS` (1 / cos(pitch)) to offset the steep camera. Pass true proportions for the shadow pass and in battle.
- Outdoors the shared vertex shader straightens upright things (`SetUpright`): x is divided by the depth of the ground point under each vertex, and that depth becomes `w` so textures interpolate to match. Changing `gl_Position.x` alone bends textures along each quad's diagonal. Rooms, battles, sprite bakes and the shadow pass leave it at 0. Anything drawn at a height with another shader (the "!" bubble) must be moved by hand with `WorldRenderer.Straighten`; lights on the ground and halos use the `Light` program, which shares the vertex shader.
- Glass that lights up after dark is marked by alpha in the art (`ArtSheet.PublicLight` 0.8, `HomeLight` 0.6; below 0.5 is cut out), never found by colour. Write such texels with `SetRaw` or `Pix.Lit`: `PixelCanvas.Set` blends anything that isn't opaque. Paint frames and mullions afterwards, opaque, over the glass.
- The view is widened for relief edge by edge (`WorldRenderer.VisibleRects`): the south edge by ground south of it that rises into it, the north edge by ground north of it that falls away. Asked as one strip, high ground in the north widened the view to the south, and chunks that lay almost under the zoomed-in camera were drawn, where the straightening of upright things divides by next to nothing (a sheet over the Battle Frontier's plaza, plan 01 · M10).
- What the art marks as lit turns into the rig's `GlowColor` at the rig's `LampGlow`, whatever its own colour: lamplight at a lamp's full strength made lava pale yellow after dark. A place whose light is its own sets both in its climate (`ArtLook.InClimate`).
- A climate's light, like snow country's, eases in over a second of the field's clock when the area changes on one map: a tool that puts the player down in such a place waits for it before a shot (the harness's `battlezone` does), or the picture shows the weather and not the climate.
- Sheet metal's ribs (`WallKind.Metal`) leave rows of lone texels wherever a band or a window's sill crosses them, which `WorldModelTests` counts: a style with bands and ribbon windows takes panels.
- A map's trees are kept in chunks of eight tiles (`MeshBatches.Chunk`) and only the chunks near the view are drawn (`WorldRenderer.VisibleRects`; shadow casters reach further toward the sun). They are drawn depth-first: a pre-pass lays down their depth with `Prepass`, then they are shaded with depth writes off, which is what keeps a forest in view within the frame budget. The vertex shader declares `invariant gl_Position` for that.
- The raylib build the game ships with (6.0, whose own GPU skinning is compiled out) binds `vertexBoneIndices` and `vertexBoneWeights` to attribute locations 7 and 8 in every shader, but its `UploadMesh` doesn't upload those buffers. `SkinnedModel.AttachBones` puts them on the mesh's vertex array itself; without it every vertex reads the default values and the whole model follows one bone. Their ids are the `SkinnedModel`'s own, freed in its `Unload`, and never written into `Mesh.VboId`: that list has seven places in this build (`MAX_MESH_VERTEX_BUFFERS`) and `UnloadMesh` frees seven, so ids kept at 7 and 8 were two writes past its end and two buffers left behind by every model let go, 2 MB a mesh (plan 03 · D13). Anything else given to a raylib mesh beyond what raylib put there is kept and freed the same way. Bone matrices go to the shaders as three `vec4` rows each (`bones[]`), never through raylib's own bone uniform.
- In an `SdfModel` a cut only carves the shapes added before it, and later shapes join what's left: sculpt hair and carve its face opening before adding the head, and hem a skirt before adding the legs. Paint can be limited to some bones (`Paint(..., onBones)`) so a sleeve ends on the arm without colouring the body beside it. A shape inside the surface so far adds nothing, without any warning: hats go on the hair's surface (about 0.3 above the head's centre), not on the head (`CharacterKitTests.EveryHatShowsOverTheHair`).
- A cut carves every shape added before it, on any bone, as far as it reaches: a second bat wing's edge cuts once carved the first wing away, and a membrane trimmed to shape by big cuts left slivers that only the outline shell showed. Build thin parts to their outline instead (`BatMembrane` fills triangles with their inscribed ellipses) and keep cuts small and local (a scallop, a mouth).
- A decal on a seam between two shapes or near a surface's edge (an eye where Shieldon's face meets its shield) leaves part of its square uncovered and fails `EachDecalCoversItsWholeSquare`: put it on one smooth curve, or paint the marking (`PaintEll`).
- A direction worked out for a part (a leaf's, a spike's) must never come to nothing: `Vector3.Normalize` of a zero vector is NaN, and one NaN point throws the whole mesh out (a leaf of Fomantis's cap pointing straight down left a stray piece and its eyes uncovered).
- A spike's squash flattens it one way only, across the local z left after aligning it with its length; a fin in the plane of the body or a leaf flat to the sky comes out a blade the wrong way round, or a balloon if rounded instead. `Blade` (in `PokemonModels.Sinnoh3.cs`) takes the way its face should look.
- A thick tube through many points (a tongue) beads at every joint with the default blend, because each smooth join adds a little: join its segments with a blend of 0. Their ends meet in the same sphere, so the surface stays smooth.
- A cut has to break the surface. One wholly inside a shape leaves a hollow there: a closed piece of mesh nobody sees, which the soundness check counts as a loose part (Magmortar's bores did, until the cut reached past the barrel's end).
- Paint recolours every shape of its bone inside it. To edge an opening in one colour and show another in it, put what is inside on a bone of its own: Snorunt's face is on its head bone, the orange round it on the body's.
- Paint colours nothing on other bones, and a serpent's body is spread over many (`Coils` makes one every two points): a stripe down its length is painted a stroke on each stretch's own bone, centred on that stretch, as `Throat` does for Dratini's line. Painted on one bone, only that stretch shows it.
- A decal only takes the triangles that mostly follow its own bone (a weight of 0.3 or more). Where the head is part of the body's own shape (Happiny's line, Sudowoodo's trunk), a face given to the head bone shows nowhere or in pieces: give its eyes and marks to the bone whose shape the face is on.
- Character outlines are a separate offset shell of the SDF (`SdfCache.Get(model, cell, iso)`) drawn with front faces culled. Pushing the body out along its normals instead pokes ink through every crease (hat brims, between locks of hair).
- A colour patch that lies close under the surface it joins (a belly, a mask, a band) stays inside the smooth blend's reach and tints a wide blur round itself: make it paint (`PokeBuilder.PaintEll`), which recolours without changing the shape. Paint needs a soft edge about two mesh cells wide (`PaintEdge`), or its border follows the vertices in steps.
- Eyes and markings are decals, never shapes: anything as small as a few mesh cells comes out a blob. A decal's square must be about twice the feature (the feature fills its middle half), so the whole triangles under it cover the feature however coarse the mesh; a square that hugs the feature loses its edges.
- Models rendered on their own (sprite bakes) must call `shaders.SetStudio()` first, or fog and cloud shade from the last scene leak into them. It also clears the walkers that grass leans away from, so `WorldRenderer` calls `SetWalkers` after the sprite bakes, and `BattleRenderer` passes none.
- Vertex alpha below 1 makes scenery sway; exactly 0 (`MeshBuilder.Sway(c, 1)`) also makes it part round walkers. Tall grass, lawn tufts and flowers are in `MeshPass.Ground`, which takes shadows but casts none.
- Field art moves in whole texels: place sprites and cards on the 1/32 grid, and step animation in the water shader with `floor`, never by scrolling UVs.
- `GameEngine.ApplySettings` re-syncs the clock, mute and quality from `Settings`. Change `engine.Settings` and call it (the harness does) rather than setting `GameClock.Fixed` directly. A quality change reloads only the render targets; shaders stay loaded because cached materials refer to them.
- Full screen is `WindowSettings`' own borderless window, one row taller than the monitor. Don't use `Raylib.ToggleBorderlessWindowed` (a build error) or size the window to the monitor exactly: the NVIDIA driver then treats the OpenGL window as exclusive full screen and the picture flickers.
- The virtual screen's alpha is written by its clear alone: `GameEngine.Draw` masks alpha writes (`Rlgl.ColorMask`) for the whole 2D pass and unmasks them after `EndMode2D`, because raylib's usual blending blends alpha like a colour, so every translucent panel lowered the picture's alpha and the window, which draws it over black, showed it darker than the harness's shots. A colour mask outlives the pass that set it: anything that changes it puts it back as it found it (`SceneMeshes`' depth pre-pass), and the harness warns (`!! the picture is see-through`) if a shot comes out otherwise.
- `Raylib.BeginScissorMode` takes real pixels of the target being drawn into, not layout units: the ×2 camera of the 2D pass doesn't apply to it, so a scissor given `VirtualWidth` and `VirtualHeight` clips to a quarter of the 4K picture.
- `UiFonts` has two faces per weight (plan 17 · N5, the Latin half): the first is Latin-1 plus the dashes, the ellipsis and the arrows (`UiFonts.Codepoints`, the atlas English has always had); the second (`UiFonts.ExtraCodepoints`: Latin Extended-A, Ș ș Ț ț, the curly and low quotes, guillemets, € and ™) is a second atlas of the same size made the first time a text holds one of its characters (`GetExtra`), so English pays nothing. `Draw` and `Measure` scan the text, and only one with an extra character is cut into runs by face (`UiFonts.Runs`) and set one after another with the tracking between runs; every drawing and measuring of text goes through them, never `Raylib.DrawTextEx` with `UiFonts.Get`. A character neither face has (Nunito lacks ĳ, ſ, the arrows, the gender marks) is drawn as "?". No-break spaces are drawn as plain spaces (`UiFonts.Spaced`). Signs the interface needs beyond that (the money mark, the gender marks, arrows) are drawn with `UiShapes`, not typed.
- Text is measured without a window by `UI/Kit/TextMetrics` (plan 16 · T8): it reads each weight's character map and advances from the font file and reckons as raylib does (an advance scaled to the atlas's 160 and cut to a whole number, the sum scaled to the size, the tracking between characters), which was held against raylib's own loader for every character `UiFonts` loads in all three weights. `ModernUi.Wrap` and a check with no window wrap by the one `TextMetrics.Wrap`, each with its own measure. It also says what can be drawn (`CanDraw`): everything either face has (a character is drawable iff the typeface has a glyph for it, `LatinFontTests`), but none of the four arrows `UiFonts.Codepoints` asks for, which Nunito doesn't have; a non-breaking space is the plain space's width.
- `RenderContext.NeedsFxaa` turns FXAA on only where the window shows the scene at more than three quarters of its size (the high preset in a window wider than 2880, the medium in one wider than 2160, the low in one wider than 1440); a smaller window anti-aliases by scaling the scene down and FXAA would cost half a millisecond for nothing.
- A frame is spent filling 4K, not on geometry (plan 04 · G11; the style guide's "Quality presets" has the numbers), so a new effect is weighed by what it adds to every pixel. Measure with the harness's `profile` mode before and after; `FrameProfiler.Lap` is how a new pass gets its own line.
- A 4K texture is slow to read at scattered places: the scene's colour and depth are each read once a frame into a half-resolution copy (`RenderContext`'s `half` and `depthHalf`), and whatever needs them small (the wide blur, the glow, ambient occlusion) reads the copy. A full-screen pass clears nothing first: every pixel is written.
- The shadow map compares for itself (`Gl.CompareDepth`; the shaders read it as a `sampler2DShadow`, compiled with `HARDWARE_PCF`), and its taps stand exactly one texel apart: further apart, each tap's ramp ends before the next begins and an edge shows as steps. `Gl` is the only place that calls OpenGL past raylib; where it finds no library the shaders fall back to the rotated Poisson disc.
- Uniforms are set through `FieldShaders`' own `Set`, which keeps each location (`FieldShaders.Location`). `Raylib.GetShaderLocation` every frame is a round trip that waits for the graphics driver, so it is a build error anywhere but `FieldShaders.Location`, which every shader's owner asks.
- A battle's stage lays its depth down before it is shaded (`SceneMeshes.PrepassEverything`): seen from the trainer's shoulder, one thing stands behind another all the way to the horizon. In every scene what stands on the ground is drawn before the ground.
- Baking a chunk's ground is mostly its masks (`PixelGround.Mask`): the box blur carries the sums of all columns side by side so memory is read a row at a time (a column at a time it cost five times as much), and a mask of nothing is null, not an array of zeros. `FrameTests` holds that the fast ways bake what the slow ones did, bit for bit. Chunks bake at a lower thread priority, those in view first (`WorldRenderer.Bake`).
- Battle timing: a line of the log starts what it describes as it appears (`Said.Shows`), and what it carries in `OnImpact` (a hit's damage, its sound) lands `HitDelay` after the lunge through the engine's pending effects, which `ConfirmMessage` flushes first. In the core, attach an event to the line that tells of it (`Say(...).With(...)`, `.AtImpact(...)`); an event emitted on its own happens between lines, as soon as the line before it has been read. Effects are timed to the same moment (`BattleAnimator.ImpactTime`, 0.35 s), so a new effect must land then, not when its own animation would like to. Cue seeds come from the animator, never from the battle's `Random`, so presentation can't change a battle's outcome.
- Battle effects and the ball's flashes are drawn with rlgl and the default shader inside the 3D pass (like the blob shadows). Quads marked `Depth` are depth-tested (rings round a body, ambient particles); the rest draw over the scene, so a burst at a Pokémon's middle isn't swallowed by its own body. None write depth.
- A sprite stretched along a direction (`FxList.Streak`) is turned to the camera and keeps its whole length on screen: a long line that runs away from the lens comes out as a spoke to the vanishing point (Trick Room's first grid was a starburst over the trees). A line of the world is a ribbon from end to end (`FxList.Ribbon`, with `depth: true` to sit in the scene).
- The `Streak` shape's line is about a sixth of its quad's width, so a streak of half-width 0.03 is a hair at 1080p: a streak that should be seen wants a half-width of 0.05 units or more. And particles that loop through a box are seen only where the box is in the lens: the battle's overview takes in about x −14 to 9, z −10 to 23 and y under 6. The weather's rain and sand (plan 25 · Y1, written where the harness couldn't run) were a handful of hairs for both reasons until Y2 widened them and drew their boxes round the lens (`ArenaFx.WeatherStreak`, `RainMin`, `RainMax`).
- Light added over a bright field washes out to white (a violet mote over the day's trees was a white dot): what should keep its colour is matter, alpha-blended, with only its halo, edge or glint added on top.
- The same holds for a tint over a body (plan 25 · Y3): a purple glow added to a brown Pokémon only brightened it. A cast that says "poisoned", "frozen" or "angry" is an alpha-blended glow in its own colour.
- What circles a Pokémon can't simply be depth-tested: a round body's own bulk reaches as far out as the circle, and all of it but the two at the sides is hidden (Fire Spin was four flames, and a band round the body nothing at all). The battle's cameras stand on the +Z side of the field, so a thing on that side of the body is drawn over it and only the rest is depth-tested (`MoveFx.Round`, and `Bands`' front arc).
- From the overview the foe's platform is seen at about 7°: a ring or a swirl lying flat there is a thread (plan 25 · Y2 found the same of a dome). A band tilts its plane toward the camera, and a whirl on the ground has upright ones standing in it (`MoveFx.Vortex`). A flat quad's own axes change with its normal (`FxRenderer.Quad` picks its reference by whether the normal is within 26° of straight up), so the turn that points one of its sides at the camera is asked of `MoveFx.TurnToward`, never worked out for a level quad and reused for a tilted one: the bands' front arcs stood at the Pokémon's side.
- A mark placed "over the head" at some share of the Pokémon's height is a long way over it on the player's own platform, which is close to the lens: 1.2 of the height put a held item's glint beside the foe's HP box. 1.1 is just clear of a head on both platforms.
- A mark set out by a Pokémon's height alone suits one shape of body (plan 09 · L2): bubbles 0.3 to 0.48 of the height out sat inside a Bidoof, which is as wide as it is tall, and floated a body's width off an Empoleon, which is a quarter as wide. What stands beside a body goes by `FxPlaces.HalfWidth`; what is over its head by its height.
- Sizes in a Pokémon's height are twice the pixels on the player's own platform that they are on the foe's, since the body is: a mark is sized for the foe's, where it is smallest (flames of 0.1 of the height were a few pixels there), and then looked at on the player's for what has grown heavy.
- A dark copy of a thin glyph drawn a fifth bigger under it is no rim: scaled about its middle its strokes part from the pale one's and the letter reads double (the sleeper's Z's did). A glyph's shadow is the same glyph at the same size, a sixth of it down and to the right; a rim by scaling suits a filled shape alone (a star, a heart).
- The `Bubble` shape is a rim and a glint with next to nothing inside: alpha-blended over leaves it is a faint ring a few pixels across. Something small that must read on any ground gets a body under it (a `Dot` at three fifths' strength, 1.3 times the bubble's size).
- A harness helper that confirms a line and looks at the next at once lands the first line's pending hit in the same frame: the flinch and the shake it reports belong to the line before. Read each line for a second before sending it on (`ReadTo` in `Modes/ConditionsMoments.cs`), which is also what brings the camera back to the overview before a turn's end.
- One kind of shot is not the same every run: a menu's sprites are loaded within four milliseconds of the computer's own clock a frame (`PokemonSprites.Service`), so a shot taken a frame after a screen first asks for several (`battle`'s `23h_battle_info_double`, a frame after the panel opens) shows two icons or four by how busy the machine is. Before blaming a change for such a shot, run the commit before it again at the same hour (plan 25 · Y3 did: the two runs agreed with one another, and both differed from the run of the same commit made an hour earlier).
- A harness helper that reads a battle on to a line (`Confirm`, then `Skip(0.3)`, until the line is the one asked for) has let 0.3 s of what that line starts play by the time it finds it, and the camera cuts from a move's user to its target 0.24 s in (`BattleCamera.CutTime`): a shot of something brief (a doll aside, a look half faded) counts from the line itself, or it shows the moment after.
- A battle effect placed by the overview camera (what keeps off the arc that faces the lens) is placed for the menu's shot: the director's other shots see it from elsewhere, so it must also look right from the side and from behind.
- Arena walls, rock faces and rooms go in `MeshPass.Ground` (shaded and shadowed, never casting), so tall scenery can't throw the platforms into shadow. Lights an arena adds (crystals, lamps, glowing rims) are `MeshPass.Light` and drawn by `BattleStage.DrawLights` at the rig's `LampGlow`.
- Place things with `Relief.At` (or `Relief.Under` for someone who may be on a bridge), never with `Map.HeightAt` (`ArchitectureTests` holds it, but for the two readers it names): the drawn height of water, of the lowlands and of a bank is not the walked one. Anything drawn on raised ground with the default shader (the blob shadows, the "!" bubble) also has to be moved by `WorldRenderer.Straighten`, because the ground it lies on has been.
- A river runs on under a bridge: `GroundBaker.IsWaterAt` counts a deck over water as water for the water mask and the water mesh, the deck's boards are painted over it afterwards, and the deck is drawn a hair above the water so a boardwalk level with its banks covers it. Leave any of the three out and the river is cut in two by a pond's rounded shore, or shows through the boards.
- Bright, pale ground blooms to a blank under the day light: snow and ice are kept well below white (the style guide's numbers), and the sprite shader tints white texels in shade, so effects like foam belong to the water, not to a sprite.
- A building of the world is styled by its model's town (`BuildingInfo.Town`), not by the map round it: pass `map.ArchitectureAt` to `BuildingArt.StyleOf` all the same, since a hand-made map's buildings have no town of their own. Its storeys come from the catalogue or the model's height; a tower or a lighthouse is a one-storey ground stage with its stages built on that (`BuildingModels.Stages`), so its style says one storey.
- A roof that isn't tiled is still asked for by colour: `BuildingArt.SnowRoof` and `MetalRoof` are colours that stand for the snow sheet and the ribbed metal sheet, which `BuildingArt.RoofTiles` returns for them. A style under snow has `RoofColor = SnowRoof`; don't add a flag beside it.
- An open porch (two sides and a canopy) must leave the way in open to the eye as it is to the feet: its side blocks stop where the canopy hangs, the canopy spans them, and the door is painted on the wall behind. One block over the whole porch row would hide whoever walks in. A closed porch has the door in its own front, and `BuildingArt.BaysOf` leaves the wall behind any porch tile blank.
- Tiles under a model that isn't a building's block take the ground of the tiles round them (`WorldMapBuilder.GroundLike`), so a lamp in a street stands on paving; a ship's tiles are water. Open tiles whose cover the import couldn't name do the same, or every unknown texture is a square of lawn in the paving. A blocked tile the original draws nothing on is forest only where trees stand within two tiles of it, and rock anywhere else (`World.BareRock`): taken for a tree everywhere, it put pines on Spear Pillar. A place whose ground shows as lawn and pines where it shouldn't is first a question of textures `tools/MapImporter/Cover.cs` doesn't sort (the importer's report lists them).
- The harness's `cities` mode reads the importer's whole output, which is not in the repository: after changing `tools/MapImporter/Cover.cs` run the importer without `--quick` before judging the towns, and with `--data` to refresh what the game ships.
- Whatever the field's life is timed by uses `FieldLife.Now`, never raylib's clock: it stops while a battle or a menu covers the field, and it is a double. Weather that moves two thousand units a second multiplies that clock, and a float's steps would show as jitter after a few hours; `WeatherFx` wraps each piece's place round its own span before rounding, and a layer of mist's offset where its cloud repeats.
- An upright quad drawn with the default shader (a bubble, dust, a leaf) has each of its corners moved by `WorldRenderer.Straighten` (`DrawUpright` given the camera). Moving only its middle leaves it leaning, the more the further it stands from the middle of the screen.
- A card that is part of a wall (an open door) is drawn with the scenery's shader and a wall's normal (`CharacterSprites.MakeCard(..., partOfAWall: true)`). With the people's shader it is shaded differently from the wall round it, and its marked texels (the doorway, lit like a window) don't glow after dark.
- A face that moves has every frame the size of the face. Its sheet is rewritten with `UpdateTextureRec` before anything is drawn in the frame (`MapScene.Animate`), and its frames are kept as images with the scene and freed in `Unload`.
- Weather over the picture is drawn after the composite, in layout units, in blocks on the three-unit grid (one texel of the field at 1080p); only mist is soft (the haze texture, bilinear and repeating). What lands (rain's flecks and rings) is in the world, through `FieldLife.Rainfall`, so it stays where it fell as the camera moves.
- A door's card and a step's print are found from the player's own tiles: `Player.Heading` is where a step in progress ends, and `GameEngine` opens the door of a warp tile as soon as the player is heading for it. `FieldLife` keeps only one door moving; going through a warp clears it with everything else.
- A chunk of a streamed map is prepared on a worker thread, so `MapScene.Prepare` must not create anything on the GPU. Every shared texture it looks up has to exist already: `MapScene.Warm` makes them on the main thread first, and `SceneTextures` throws if a new one is first asked for anywhere else.
