Scenery that needs a new shared texture goes into `Warm` too.
- Chunks are baked alone and in any order. Anything random in the ground or on it (noise, tufts, which tree) must be seeded by the tile's place in the world, and `PixelGround` bakes two tiles past the chunk's edge and crops them off (`ChunkPad`), or the borders show as seams. `WorldTests.AChunksGroundIsTheSameHoweverMuchIsBakedWithIt` checks the ground and the water mask.
- A chunk's scene is freed with `MapScene.Unload`: its meshes and the textures baked for it. Never `UnloadMaterial` there (a build error); raylib would free the shared textures and shaders the material points at.
- A sculptor must never change its `PokeGenome`: genomes are cached and shared, and a sculpt that changes between builds changes its mesh cache key and its sprite signature. Draw anything random in a sculpt from the sculptor's own `GenomeRandom`, never `System.Random` (whose numbers may change between .NET versions; `ArchitectureTests` holds it).
- `PokemonModels.Get` meshes a model that isn't built yet on the calling thread (a second or more). Code that shows a species ask for it ahead with `Request` and only waits behind a fade; a frame must never be the first to ask for a model. Menus use the baked sprites, which never wait.
- Tests that change `ModelOverrides.Folders` belong to the `Model files` collection, which runs alone: every model built meanwhile would read the swapped folders.
- Rock's lip (`WorldMapBuilder.CaveLipRise`) must be no lower than `Relief.WeldLimit`. A step under three quarters of a tile is joined to its neighbours by a slope, so a lower lip made the floor beside every wall rise to meet it, and whoever walked there with it.
- What a tile under a model shows is guessed from the ground round it (`WorldMapBuilder.GroundLike`), and that guess must never be taken from a tile the import couldn't name: such a tile reads as lawn until it has taken its own ground, and put squares of lawn under coal heaps in a paved yard. Made or bare ground at a distance wins over lawn at the same distance.
- A prop a model of the world placed covers its model's box, and the world says which of those tiles block: a turbine's blades turn over grass one walks through, a conveyor spans open ground. Only props without a `Model` block every tile they cover (`Prop.IsSolid`), so code that asks "is the way blocked" asks the map, never the prop.
- The world is no longer one place with one way through it: a cave lies between Route 204's halves, rocks wait for Rock Smash, the Ironworks is reached by Surf. A test that asks whether something can be come to walks the whole game through its warps (`WorldWalk` in the test project), not one map from one tile; it passes people a new game hides until their scene comes (`WorldWalk.HiddenAtStart`: the rival on his doorstep), and a test of who stands where leaves them out.
- The old hand-made maps live on as fixtures of the art-kit tests (`Fixtures.Names`: eight, Jubilife City and the stand-in Pallet Town among them). `MapDatabase.Get` of such a name gives the map of Sinnoh, so a test that means the old map asks `Fixtures`.
- Something small standing right behind the player is behind the player's own head: an upright sprite covers more than two tiles of ground beyond its feet from the field's steep camera. An item's ball one tile north of the player isn't seen at all, in the original either, so the harness looks at one from the side (`Beside` in its `story` mode), and a shot that shows nothing where a ball should be is first a question of where the player stands.
- The original numbers its hidden items by their flags, not by their place in its table: the list of flags has unused entries the table skips, so the nth entry is not hidden item n (`DecompMaps.ParseHiddenItems`). Reading it by position looked right for the first few dozen and then gave places the wrong items.
- An item's ball can stand in a passage one tile wide, and what lies beyond is reached only by picking it up: a test that asks what can be come to walks through item balls (`WorldWalk`).
- `Map.NPCs` is who is on the map now. Whatever must reach everyone the map has (saving which trainers are beaten, preloading character models, writing a map file back, finding someone for a script) asks `Map.Everyone`, or the people a flag has hidden are forgotten.
- **Who stands where is asked by place, never by going through everyone** (plan 01 · M12): `Map.NPCs` is a `PeopleList` (`Overworld/PeopleList.cs`): a list in the order people were put on the map that also keeps them by the square of eight tiles they stand in, kept up as people are added, taken off and moved (`NPC.GridX` and `GridY` tell the lists they are in). `Map.GetNpcAt`, `NpcIn` and `IsWalkable` ask `NPCs.At(x, y)`, and whatever looks at people each frame asks `NPCs.Near(x, y, reach, into)` or `In(...)` with a list of its own: only people within `Wandering.Reach` (64 tiles) of the player wander or have their bubbles counted down, a trainer's eyes are asked only of those within `TrainerApproach.SightReach`, the berry soil within sight, and the renderer draws from `WorldRenderer.PeopleInSight`. `Map.Warps` is a `TrackedList<Warp>` whose `Version`, with `Warp.Moves`, tells `GetWarpAt` and `WarpsAt` when their table by tile is stale. A loop over `map.NPCs` or `map.Warps` in anything that runs every frame or every step is a walk through all of Sinnoh: `MapIndexTests` holds the indexed answers equal to the scanned ones on the whole world, with people moving, hidden and shown and warps shut and opened.
- A script's names mean the people of its own place: `Map.FindPerson(name, place)` finds nobody of another area, because the map of Sinnoh has a `clown_1` and a `pokemon_breeder_f` in more than one town and the first one found was once the wrong one, far out of sight. A script written outside the files (the harness's scene) is parsed under the name of the area it plays in.
- A script must never be left waiting on something that can't happen: a host method that starts something the player sees through has to change the game's state or set what `Busy` reads in the same call, and a walk the player can't take is given up (`PlayerWalk.GiveUpAfter`). A script that throws (`ScriptException`) is ended with a notice and the game goes on.
- Tests that play scripts on a place's map use maps of their own (`StoryTests.OwnMaps`, or `MapFile.ToMap()`): a script walks people about, beats trainers and takes people off the map, and `MapDatabase`'s maps are everybody's. `EveryWayThrough` puts people back between one way through a script and the next.
- The original's item table keeps a medicine's HP as a signed byte: all, half and a quarter are -1, -2 and -3, and a Hyper Potion's 200 is -56. The importer reads it so (`hpRestored`); read as a plain number it made a Hyper Potion take 56 HP away.
- A held item's rule lives where the original reads it, found by hold effect (`BattleEffects.HoldEffectOf`), never by the item's name: a line of the formula or the core, or a hook in `HeldItemEffects`. An item compared by name (`HeldItem?.Name == "Griseous Orb"`) is right only where the original names the item itself (Giratina's orb, the plates' species checks).
- Never bake or build the map of Sinnoh whole (`MapScene.Build`, `PixelGround.Bake(map, margin, …)`): it is 960×960 tiles and takes gigabytes. Because `MapDatabase.Get` falls back to it, code that takes a map by name can get it by accident; this is how the test run once hung. Tests that need small outdoor maps load the six old hand-made ones from `PokemonPlatinumTests/Fixtures/maps` (`Fixtures.Map`, `Fixtures.Any`).
- A face of an `ArtSheet` is at most 2046 texels wide, which is 63 tiles. A room's back wall, the top of it and the kerb before its floor are therefore laid in lengths of 32 tiles (`MapScene.AddInterior`; a room that fits in one length keeps the art keys it had), or a hall as wide as the headquarters' lower basement (85 tiles) throws as its scene is built. `GalacticHeadquartersTests.ARoomsWallsAreLaidInLengthsTheArtSheetHolds` builds it.
- A room is looked at from its middle, and a long one is followed down its length. One wider than 30 floor tiles (`WorldRenderer.WidestRoom`) is followed across its width too, the camera's target kept within the room by half of what it shows (`HallInView`); the shadow's focus goes with the camera there, or the far end of the hall falls outside the shadow map.
- The Distortion World's rock stands three quarters of a tile above its islands (`WorldMapBuilder.VoidRimRise`), and the original puts things on tiles the import knows only as blocked, which are that rock here. A boulder pushed onto the tile it falls from climbed the rim and stood on it, and Mesprit, hung over a rim at the island's height, was drawn inside it: a pit's tile is made nothing (`PlaceEvents`), and whoever has a `level` over a rim stands on the rim (`Relief.Under` takes the higher of the two).
- Something that stands right behind whoever faces it is behind their head (above): Giratina in its room hangs at a `level` most of a tile over the stones for that reason, under `FieldMovement.StepLimit`, past which it could no longer be spoken to.
- A scene's own `hide` and `show` hold until the map is come to again, whatever the flags say meanwhile: a tool that reads who is on a map after a scene elsewhere has set their flag comes to the map first (`ArriveOnMap`), or it reports someone who is gone as there (the harness's `pillar` did, of Looker and of a boulder).
- A test or a tool that waits for a scene to change maps reads on through what is still being said: text left on the screen holds its script, and the warp behind it never comes (the harness's `OnTo`).
- A tool that puts the player down in the middle of a place (the harness's `At`) has not come to it as far as the game knows: the first step taken there counts as walking in and goes to the place's own arrival script, which comes before the step's wild Pokémon and its triggers. The harness's `encounters` mode lost the Poké Radar's patch that way the night Route 202 was given an `OnEnter`: such a mode calls `Driver.EnterArea` first.
- Anything a tile south of the player that stands taller than about nine tenths of a tile is drawn over their feet, and something as tall as the player hides them to the waist: the field's camera is steep, and an upright sprite covers more ground beyond its feet than it is tall. A follower drawn taller than `GameEngine.TallStroller` keeps a tile further back for that (`Follower.Gap`).
- A held face's frames (plan 09 · L12) differ only inside its rim. The sheet repeats the first frame's edge texels once all round a face, and only the face's own rectangle is redrawn when its frame changes, so a frame with another edge shows the first frame's as a line beside it (`SetPieceTests` holds the tray's rim the same in all thirteen). And a face's key is the face: two PCs that shared `monitor.front` would both come on, which is why a PC that is used has a screen keyed by its tile.
- What stands behind a counter shows only the top of its front from the room's camera: the healing machine's console is hidden below the middle of its screen, so what that screen shows while the machine works is drawn in its upper half. Look at a new face in a room's shot before trusting its painter's picture of it.
- With nothing telling it anything, the camera's rig must change nothing, to the bit, or every shot of every mode moves by a texel somewhere: `CameraRig.Fov` hands a lens back untouched at a zoom of exactly 1, `Look` gives the player's own point when the camera hasn't been sent, a pan and a release keep the arithmetic they always had (the player's point plus the eased share of the way to the spot), and a room's target is clamped and snapped to texels only where it was before or a zoom gives it leave (`ZoomSlack` above nought): snapping a room's middle that was never snapped moves the whole room. `CameraRigTests.ARigNobodyToldAnythingLeavesTheCameraAsItIs` holds the first two; the harness's `diff` of `life`, `story` and `title` holds the rest.
- The camera's clock is not the field's: `WorldRenderer.RunCamera` is run by the engine beside `FieldLife.Advance`, with the frame's own time, while the field's is handed `FieldTime(dt)`. Whatever a scene's camera is timed by (a pan, a path, a zoom, the bars, slow motion's own length) goes by the camera's clock, so a script's `wait` and a camera move mean the same seconds under slow motion; whatever the field does goes by `FieldLife.Now` or the time it is handed. A move of the rig begun in a frame is first advanced by that frame's render, as a pan always was: advancing it in `Update` before the script runs would make every pan a frame late and move the shots that catch one half way.
- A lens zoomed out sees past the block of three chunks by three kept ready round the camera, where a building that belongs to a chunk further off can still reach into the picture's edge: `ScenesFor` is handed the ground the widest lens ahead will show and asks for every chunk whose reach touches it (`ChunksUnder`), from the moment the zoom is told, and the shadow map is spread over the wider view. A lens wider than `CameraRig.WidestZoom` would want more than that.
- A way into a battle that moves the camera (plan 09 · L14) holds a lens over the rig's own zoom and never sets the zoom: a script that zoomed in before a legendary's battle finds its zoom where it left it once the lens is let go. Whatever asks what the camera shows asks `CameraRig.Lens`, not `Zoom` (the field of view, the room's slack, the shadow's reach), or it is a step behind the picture through the rush; and the held step is added to the built camera by the renderer, never to `Look`, which the field's straightening and everything a script's camera does go by.
- A way into a battle takes its own time since plan 09 · L13 (`SceneTransition.OutSeconds`), and the harness counts its clock in frames: give a way another lead or closing, or a battle another way, and every shot a mode takes after such a battle is taken at another moment of the clock. `diff` then lists them all, for the sway of grass and flowers, people's idle frames and the text box's arrow, and for nothing else: look at a board before believing more has moved (L13 itself moved `windworks` after its first grunt and `tower` after its first battle that way). The pictures of a closing are `life`'s, at its end, and a battle on grass or in a room still closes in nine tenths of a second. A Gym Leader's battle, which did too, opens on its band since plan 09 · L15 (3.45 s, and 3.15 s for the Elite Four's and the Champion's, where it was 0.9 s): every mode whose scripts begin one (`gyms`, `league`, `jubilife`, `veilstone`, `snowpoint`, `sunyshore`, `legends`, `epilogue`) takes its later shots at another moment of the clock.
- A portrait tinted dark is not a silhouette: a texture drawn with a dark tint is the picture at that share of its light, and a pale face shows grey in it against the clothes. A band's silhouette is the picture's alpha alone in one colour (`SceneTransition.Bands.cs`'s flat shader). And the studio's light (`CharacterStudio.Light`, high and to the left) is for a whole figure: on a bust it leaves the jaw and the neck in a band of the skin's shade that reads as a beard, so `RenderBust` lights from the front. And a Gym Leader is dressed in their Gym's colours, which are their band's: laid straight on the band, a bust loses its coat in it (Gardenia's jacket, Bertha's cardigan, Flint's hair), which is what the card of the band's light colour behind each portrait is for. A band's colours are judged with its person on it: `gyms` and `league` shoot all thirteen (`SHOTS_FILTER=band`).
- What rides over a way into a battle (a ball, a mark) is what `SceneTransition.Pieces` says for the kind and the moment, and `Draw` only draws it: a piece worked out inside the drawing can't be held still by a test, and its dark (`CoversByWay`) and its shapes (`DrawDark`) are written twice, so they are changed together. A mark that fades in is drawn with its rim under it at the same alpha, and a shape that must be exact at the picture's edges (a ring, an iris, a wedge) reaches from the furthest corner (`Reach`), never from half the diagonal: a ring that closes on a player near one side would have the far corners dark from its first frame.
- A Pokémon that is hit flickers: from the impact on (`BattleAnimator.ImpactTime`, 0.35 s after the move's line appears) it is drawn only on alternate tenths of `HitTime` (`BattleRenderer.Appear`). A shot of "X used Y!" taken later than that can show an empty platform and nothing wrong: the harness's `formats` mode shoots a move's own line a quarter of a second in, and the line about how it did once the flicker is over.
- A scene skipped must not put its walkers on their last tiles (plan 12 · Q20): the player's scripted steps are the player's own, so a walk "ended where it would end" is one walked, and walked when the scene would have walked it. A hurry that only dropped its waits left a `walk` unfinished when the next line turned the player, and the walk, finished later, turned them back (Canalave's library, found by the harness's `skips` mode: the headless host, where a walk is over as it is told, can't show it). Time a hurry doesn't wait goes to `IScriptHost.Elapse`, and a wait for walks to `Arrive`.
- The harness's game starts from the default options every run (`new GameEngine(new GameSettings())`), because since plan 12 · Q20 the game writes `settings.json` beside its saves when a scene is first seen, which for the harness is its out dir: read back, a run would begin with whatever the run before had set. A tool that makes an engine of its own does the same.
- A tool that plays a scene twice in a room puts the room's people back between the two (their tiles and facings): whoever a scene walks about a hand-made room stays where it left them until the room's own script moves them, and a second telling that begins there walks them through the walls (the `skips` mode's first run reported three people at tiles off the map).

## Project rules

- **Follow Platinum**, not Diamond/Pearl, wherever they differ (gym order, encounter tables, HM locations, music roles).
- **One set of rules, the modern ones; Platinum's are the baseline and must not move by accident** (the user's ruling of 2026-10-12, plan 06 · R19): every game is played by the modern rules, and what stays Platinum's is the content (Sinnoh's story, maps, encounter tables, trainers). Anything that differs between the generations goes behind the `Ruleset`, and the Platinum preset keeps giving what it gave (`battles-platinum.txt` holds it) until R30 decides whether it goes.
- **Nothing taken from the games**: no sprites, models, textures, music, cries or script text. Art, sound and dialogue are our own; dialogue follows the original beats in our own words. Free-licensed assets (OFL, CC0, MIT) are allowed with the user's OK before downloading, and each gets a line in `docs/art/CREDITS.md`.
- **Layouts and numbers are imported; art is not.** The importers may read where things are and what they are called: tile behaviours, heights, positions, the short names of models and textures, a model's bounding box. They never write a vertex, a texture, a palette, a sound or a line of dialogue into the repository, and their caches stay git-ignored.
- **`.gitignore` ignores every folder called `overrides`** (hand-made art next to the game), except `tools/DataImporter/Overrides/`, which is re-included by name. Check `git status` after adding a file under any other `Overrides` folder.
- **Reference data**: the pret/pokeplatinum decompilation has most game data as JSON. `docs/plans/README.md` lists the paths; fetch raw files from `https://raw.githubusercontent.com/pret/pokeplatinum/main/<path>`. Bulbapedia blocks automated fetching; pokemondb.net and Serebii work.
- **Style guide first**: when a visual rule has to change, change `docs/art/style-guide.md` before the code. `StyleGuideTests` checks the rules that can be tested without a GPU.
- **Add tests for new rules** (movement, scripts, battle effects, data completeness) and keep them green.
- **The full suite runs once, after everything is merged.** When a session does several steps at once (one of each plan, often by agents in worktrees of their own), each step runs only the tests of what it touches (`--filter`); the whole of `dotnet test PokemonPlatinumTests` runs once, on the tree with every step merged, and what fails there is fixed there. Running it per step costs the most time and finds nothing the merged run doesn't.
- **Commits**: commit or push only when the user asks; commit on `main`.
